34	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
35	
36	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
37	
38	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
39	
40	
41	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74, a non-parametric measure equivalent to Mann-Whitney $U$; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 4

3	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. 65	
66	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
67	
68	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}).
69

Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}

70	\begin{figure}[t]
71	\centering
72	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
73	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
74	\label{fig:task_specificity}
75	\end{figure}
76	
77	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.
78	
79	This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
80	
81	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M \citep{biderman2023pythia} seed calibration yields a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-associated divergence, though matched

  "old_string": "\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nHaving established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \\emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%; $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}",
  "new_string": "\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nHaving established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \\emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\\%; $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail})."

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
4	
5	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
6	
7	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
8	
9	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. Normalizing by $J_{\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attribute

1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\begin{table}[t]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{Model zoo summary \citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}
9	\label{tab:model_zoo}
10	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
11	\toprule
12	Architecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\
13	\midrule
14	GatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\
15	RetNet & Multi-scale exp.\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\
16	HGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\
17	\midrule
18	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\%, 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, 25\%} \\
19	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
25	
26	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\%, h12-1 = 7.7\%, h24-1 = 4\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.
27	
28	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
29	
30	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy (informal; not pre-registered) was fixed prior to analysis following standard catego

72	
73	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
74	
75	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}).
76	
77	\begin{figure*}[t]
78	\centering
79	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
80	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
81	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
82	\end{figure*}
83	
84	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n =

Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}\n\\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}"
  }
}
```

_source line 924 · 2026-05-25T11:14:05.263000+00:00_

[飞书用户 用户]: ⚠️ 修正：论文中的仓库链接必须用匿名链接

之前发的消息里给的是 GitHub 仓库地址，但论文里不能直接用 GitHub 链接。GitHub 仓库即使 org 名叫 anonymous，reviewer 仍然可以通过 org 页面、成员列表等途径追溯身份，不符合匿名审稿要求。

---

你这个项目的两个链接：

  论文中使用（匿名链接）：
  https://anonymous.4open.science/r/entrainment_gated_ft-9444/

  推送代码使用（GitHub 仓库）：
  https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft

---

区分用途：

1. 论文脚注里的 \url{} 必须填匿名链接（anonymous.4open.science），这是 reviewer 看到的唯一入口
2. 日常 git push 代码、整理仓库、用 PAT 推送 —— 还是用 GitHub 仓库地址，这个不变
3. anonymous.4open.science 会自动从 GitHub 仓库同步，你只要保证 GitHub 仓库内容是最新的就行

请立刻检查论文：

1. 找到脚注里的仓库链接
2. 如果写的是 github.com 开头的地址，替换成上面的匿名链接
3. 确认脚注格式是之前修正过的标准格式（整句写在脚注里面，Abstract 原文不动）

正确示例：

... (Abstract 最后一句原文)\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/entrainment_gated_ft-9444/}.}.

完成后请汇报：论文脚注中的链接是否已替换为匿名链接？

_source line 928 · 2026-05-25T11:14:20.356784+00:00_

38	\begin{abstract}
39	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/entrainment_gated_ft}.}.
40	\end{abstract}

_source line 946 · 2026-05-25T11:47:16.389034+00:00_

16	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
17

Benchmarks ordered by $\alpha$.}

95	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.
96	
97	This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
98	
99	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). _source line 1024 · 2026-05-25T11:54:39.655917+00:00_

86	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}).
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jaccard_hierarchy.pdf}
91	\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) is $2\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}
92	\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	Each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). Readers can verify $\rho$ directly from Table~\ref{tab:convergence}; with $n = 3$ architectures, all bootstrap CIs are heuristic indicators of direction rather than precise coverage.
96

  "old_string": "Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \\textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue, locking in architecture-specific sensitivity patterns. \\textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual per-example divergence arising from incomplete convergence. Appendix~\\ref{app:transformer_entrainment} shows that h3-1 correct sets indeed shift toward the Transformer baseline, providing partial support for interpretation (b). Disentangling these accounts requires ablation of the attention mechanism itself, for example replacing learned attention weights with random projections. Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) is consistent with independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing a variance-reduction trend despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.",
  "new_string": "Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \\textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue. \\textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual divergence from incomplete convergence. Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers."
}

_source line 1032 · 2026-05-25T11:55:36.728005+00:00_

22	
23	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes, and $\alpha$ does not correlate with task accuracy ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). This within-multiple-choice invariance suggests that per-example multiplicity is associated with the cognitive demands of the task rather than the mechanical constraints of the evaluation format, though the confound between task type and format cannot be fully resolved with the present benchmarks (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types (or evaluation formats), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
24	
25	\begin{figure*}[t]
26	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
27	\centering
28	\small
29	\captionof{table}{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets. $\rho_\alpha$: Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and $\alpha$. Ordered by $\alpha$.}
30	\label{tab:multiplicity}
31	\vspace{2pt}

_source line 1036 · 2026-05-25T11:55:57.654971+00:00_

82	
83	
84	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
85	
86	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}).

Based on the search results, I found the paper you're looking for:

"Predictive Multiplicity in Classification" by Charles Marx, Flavio du Pin Calmon, and Berk Ustun was published at ICML 2020.

The paper defines predictive multiplicity as the ability of a prediction problem to admit competing models with conflicting predictions, and introduces measures to evaluate the severity of predictive multiplicity, along with integer programming tools to compute these measures exactly for linear classification problems.

I found the information you were searching for. The paper "Choice of Plausible Alternatives: An Evaluation of Commonsense Causal Reasoning" was authored by Melissa Roemmele, Cosmin Adrian Bejan, and Andrew S. Gordon, and was published in the AAAI Spring Symposium on Logical Formalizations of Commonsense Reasoning in 2011.

The authors created one thousand English-language questions that directly assess commonsense causal reasoning, called the Choice Of Plausible Alternatives (COPA) evaluation.

[Audit 回复]:
[PAPER AUDIT] entrainment_gated_ft 论文质量审计报告

━━━ 确定性检查 ━━━
编译: ✅
页数: 22
源码: 2 critical, 100 warning

━━━ 内容结构 ━━━
- [warning] [叙事一致性] introduction.tex:26 Introduction 正文将 contribution 2（per-example overlap stabilization）定性为 "A supporting observation"，但随后的贡献列表（第29-33行）将其提升为正式 contribution item #2。二者的定位不一致——如果是 supporting observation，不应在贡献列表中与核心发现并列；如果是独立贡献，正文不应降格为 "supporting"。建议统一措辞：要么去掉 "supporting" 改为同等地位的 finding，要么在贡献列表中明确标注其验证性质。
- [warning] [叙事一致性] Abstract (main.tex:39) 设置了 "three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales"，但随即在同一句话中跳到 "all $p < .01$ across five architectures"。五架构验证仅在 340M 进行（§4.3 + Appendix G），属于 secondary analysis，但 Abstract 没有区分主分析（三架构 1.3B）和验证分析（五架构 340M），可能误导读者以为主分析就使用了五种架构。建议在 Abstract 中加 "including two additional architectures in validation" 或类似限定。
- [warning] [叙事一致性] conclusion.tex:5 "Per-example overlap stabilizes at 4\% attention" 去掉了 Abstract 和 Experiments 中使用的 hedging（"appears to stabilize"/"suggesting"）。鉴于统计限制（n=5 ratios、仅描述性统计、Spearman-based inferences explicitly labeled as exploratory），Conclusion 不应比 Abstract 更强。应恢复 "appears to" hedging。
- [warning] [Claim强度] conclusion.tex:6 "complementarity ($+7$--$14$\,pp oracle gain)" 这个数值范围未在正文任何表格或公式中显式给出。Experiments §4.1 仅报告了 LAMBADA 的 oracle=65.5%，未报告其他 benchmark 的 oracle 值，也没有跨 benchmark 的 oracle gain 汇总。读者无法从现有表格直接验证 "+7--14 pp" 这个范围。建议在 Appendix D13 的 complementarity decomposition 中补充各 benchmark 的 oracle gain 数据，或在 Conclusion 中仅引用已报告的具体数字。
- [info] [章节结构] 论文无独立 Discussion section。Conclusion（两短段）兼具 discussion 功能（两种解释、complementarity 含义），对于 EMNLP 短论文格式可接受，但如果正文还有页面余量，一段独立的 Discussion 段（提升解读层次、避免 Conclusion 只复述数字）会增强论文深度。
- [info] [贡献-范围] Routing case study 在 §4.1 占一个完整段落并报告四个 benchmark 的定量结果，但不在贡献列表中。论文将其定位为 "illustrative case study"，这个定位合理，但其篇幅和详细程度（包括10-fold CV、p-value）超出了 "illustrative" 的预期。如果空间紧张，可考虑将部分 routing 细节移入 appendix。
- [info] [实验严谨性] 所有模型为 single training run（在 Limitations 中已声明）。论文用 40 个 architecture-benchmark 组合的一致性（all p < .01）和 within-seed Jaccard gap（2.7-4.3×）来缓解 seed confound，这种论证方式合理但不能完全替代 multi-seed 实验。已在 Limitations 中恰当声明。
- [info] [可复现性] method.tex:41 lm-evaluation-harness 版本写为 "v0.4.x"，小版本号用 "x" 模糊处理。不同 .4.x 子版本的 benchmark 实现可能有差异（尤其 prompt template）。建议 pin 到具体版本号（如 v0.4.5）。
- [info] [符号] $\bar{J}$ 在不同上下文中的使用有轻微不一致：Table 2 使用 $\bar{J}_h$ 和 $\bar{J}_u$ 区分 helped/hurt，§4.2 的正文有时仅用 $\bar{J}$ 表示 helped+hurt 的均值。语义可从上下文推断，但统一使用或在首次出现时声明 convention 会更清晰。
- [info] [去AI] appendix.tex:653 "Notably, the $\alpha$ inflation does not track accuracy loss" — "Notably" 是 filler word，可直接删除不影响语义。
- [info] [Limitations] Limitations 段 ~162 words，覆盖 4 个不同维度（seed confound、format confound、coverage、pre-training only），实质性强。符合 EMNLP 2026 规则（约 3 个点，不需太长）。✓ Pass
- [info] [内部信息] 未发现内部实验编号、服务器路径、内部基础设施标识或语义级泄露。匿名仓库 URL 中的 "entrainment_gated_ft" 是标准匿名提交做法。✓ Pass
- [info] [写作规则] Abstract ~170 words，未超过 200 word 限制。✓ Pass
- [info] [写作规则] Limitations section 仅一个，位于正文（main.tex:52），Appendix 中无 limitations section。✓ Pass
- [info] [写作规则] ARR Responsible NLP Checklist 未包含在 PDF 中（arr_checklist.tex 存在但未被 main.tex \input）。符合 EMNLP 2026 规则（checklist 在 OpenReview form 中手动填写）。✓ Pass
- [info] [审稿规则] 页数：Phase 1 报告总共 22 页（含 appendix + references）。正文主体（Abstract 至 Conclusion）预计 7-8 页，需编译确认 ≤8 页。Limitations 和 ethical considerations 不计入 8 页限制。

━━━ 视觉排版 ━━━
- [warning] [page 1] [写作规则] Abstract 视觉估计远超 200 words 限制。Abstract 填满左栏（约 40 行）并延伸至右栏约 55% 处才到 "1 Introduction" 标题，目测约 350-450 words，约为 EMNLP 2026 的 200-word 上限的 1.7-2.2 倍。需从 LaTeX 源码精确计数确认。
- [warning] [page 22] 末页约 85% 空白：仅 Section Z "Use of AI Assistants"（3 个 bullet point）占页面顶部约 15% 面积，其余全部空白。建议将该节合并到前一页末尾，或调整 appendix 结构消除整页空白。
- [info] [page 4] Figure 2 框架图中方框内文字标签（"helped"/"hurt"/"unchanged" 等分类标注及箭头注释）字号偏小，约为正文字号的 60-65%，接近可读性下限。建议将图中关键标签字号提升至正文的 75%+ 或在 caption 中重述关键标签含义。
- [info] [page 7] Figure 7 散点图的 axis tick labels 和 legend 字号偏小（尤其是四个子面板各自的 Spearman ρ 标注），缩小打印或屏幕浏览时可能需要费力辨认。
- [info] [page 14] 单页内排列 6 个小表格（Tables 8-13），视觉拥挤。各表格间距极小，reviewer 快速浏览 appendix 时容易混淆。可考虑合并相关表格或适当分页。
- [info] [page 6] Figure 5 热力图使用单一蓝色色阶（浅蓝→深蓝），灰度打印时中间值（0.08-0.12 区间）可能难以区分。建议使用发散色阶或增加数值标注对比度。
- [info] [page 11-21] Appendix 包含 A-Z 共 26 个 section 和 28+ 个表格（Table 4 至 Table 31），体量庞大。建议在 appendix 首页增加简短目录或交叉引用索引，帮助 reviewer 快速定位感兴趣的分析。

━━━ 总计 ━━━
Critical: 2 | Warning: 106 | Info: 17

===== 行动指引 =====

1. Critical 必须修：叙事漂移→统一全文叙事，占位符→立即清理，数字不一致→核对 registry，内部信息泄露→替换为描述性名称
2. Warning 建议修：去 AI 风格→批量修改，排版问题→调整 float/空白

叙事不一致的论文比有 typo 的论文更难被接收。先统一叙事，再处理其他问题。

_source line 1148 · 2026-05-25T13:10:26.333178+00:00_

24	We present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA \citealp{paperno2016lambada}, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag \citealp{zellers2019hellaswag}, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA \citealp{bisk2020piqa}, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy \citealp{clark2018arc}, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap via Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central finding: architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks diverge systematically at the per-example level, and this divergence is established by minimal attention.
25	
26	Our central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A supporting observation is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}).
27	
28	Our contributions are as follows:
29	\begin{itemize}
30	    \item Architectural variation in hybrid recurre

_source line 1150 · 2026-05-25T13:12:21.401032+00:00_

20	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
21	
22	Prior work on predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether it extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score similarly in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the backbone choice carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
23	
24	We present the first controlled within-family per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0--25\%) and two scales (340M, 1.3B). We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA \citealp{paperno2016lambada}, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag \citealp{zellers2019hellaswag}, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA \citealp{bisk2020piqa}, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy \citealp{clark2018arc}, $N = 2{,}37
…[truncated 2373 chars]

1	\vspace{-2pt}
2	\section{Conclusion}
3	\vspace{-2pt}
4	
5	We have shown that hybrid linear recurrent architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks diverge substantially at the per-example level. This multiplicity varies by task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap stabilizes at 4\% attention (pairwise Jaccard $\leq 0.10$); two interpretations (architecture-specific fingerprints or rapid convergence toward Transformer-like behavior) remain open (Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}). Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\%.
6	At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, aggregate equivalence does not guarantee behavioral equivalence. Low cross-architecture Jaccard creates complementarity ($+7$--$14$\,pp oracle gain), though routing exploitation remains task-dependent ($+5.3$\,pp on LAMBADA; $-0.6$\,pp on PIQA).
7

36	\maketitle
37	
38	\begin{abstract}
39	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/entrainment_gated_ft-9444/}.}.
40	\end{abstract}
41

702	
703	\section{Complementarity Decomposition}\label{app:complementarity}
704	
705	We decompose the three-architecture vote on each example into four categories: unanimous correct (3/3), majority correct (2/3), minority correct (1/3), and unanimous wrong (0/3). This decomposition explains the structural failure of majority vote despite substantial per-example complementarity.
706	
707	Table~\ref{tab:complementarity} reports the decomposition at h3-1 (25\% attention). Majority vote benefits only from 2/3 examples (where the correct answer holds a majority) but is actively harmed by 1/3 examples (where the correct architecture is outvoted). On LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, the MV exploitation ratio---the fraction of complementary examples where MV selects correctly---hovers near 51\%, indicating a near-even split between MV-exploitable and MV-hostile disagreements. The oracle, by contrast, exploits both categories, explaining the large oracle--MV gap ($+7$--$14$\,pp).
708

726	This near-50\% exploitation ratio is structurally invariant across attention ratios: on LAMBADA, the ratio ranges from 51.1\% to 54.4\% across all five configurations; on HellaSwag, from 50.3\% to 51.6\%. 728	\section{Seed-Variation Calibration}\label{app:seed_calibration}
729	
730	To contextualize the $3\times$ within- vs.\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}), we compare against within-seed variation using the Pythia 70M \citep{biderman2023pythia} PolyPythias models \citep{vanderwal2025polypythias}, which provide 9 individually trained seeds of the same architecture.
731	
732	On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), within-seed mean Jaccard is $\bar{J}_h = 0.273$ (helped) and $\bar{J}_u = 0.393$ (hurt), compared to our cross-architecture reference $J \approx 0.10$. On ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), within-seed $\bar{J}_h = 0.312$ and $\bar{J}_u = 0.354$, versus cross-architecture $J \approx 0.092$. Within-seed ambiguity is $\alpha = 0.36$ (LAMBADA) and $\alpha = 0.49$ (ARC-Easy), compared to cross-architecture $\alpha = 0.30$ and $0.21$ respectively.
733	
734	Seeds thus produce comparable or higher amounts of per-example disagreement ($\alpha$) but on more overlapping examples (higher $J$). Cross-architecture variation is associated with per-example effects on less overlapping examples (lower $J$), consistent with architecture-associated divergence yielding qualitatively different per-example patterns than seed noise. The within-seed Jaccard is 2.7--4.3$\times$ higher than cross-architecture Jaccard across both benchmarks.
735

_source line 1192 · 2026-05-25T13:14:53.241318+00:00_

38	\begin{abstract}
39	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures (three primary, two validation)). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/entrainment_gated_ft-9444/}.}.
40	\end{abstract}

Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent.' | wc -w"
  }
}
```

### Tool result

_source line 1196 · 2026-05-25T13:15:03.131114+00:00_

26	Our central finding is that architectural variation produces structured predictive multiplicity whose magnitude depends on task type, not attention ratio. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), varies $2\times$ across task types but only 0.03 within each benchmark across attention ratios; pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across all ten five-architecture pairs). A second finding is that per-example overlap appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat at higher ratios. Even on LAMBADA, where aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; at 1.3B only), per-example Jaccard does not change (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}).

### Tool result

_source line 1198 · 2026-05-25T13:15:03.483446+00:00_

5	We have shown that hybrid linear recurrent architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks diverge substantially at the per-example level. This multiplicity varies by task type ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention (pairwise Jaccard $\leq 0.10$); two interpretations (architecture-specific fingerprints or rapid convergence toward Transformer-like behavior) remain open (Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}). Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74), with rescue thresholds architecture-invariant but disruption thresholds varying 47\%.

73	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
74	
75	We decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270) and WinoGrande (1{,}267) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the recurrent--hybrid contrast; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). Each example is classified as:

10	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
11	
12	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
13	
14	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}
4	The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \citep{fu2023hungry} and Hyena \citep{poli2023hyena} establish early design points in the hybrid space with gated convolutions and subquadratic operators. Mamba-2 \citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \citep{basu2026routing}. Recent theoretical work establishes a provable expressiveness hierarchy between hybrid and pure linear attention \citep{ye2026provable}, providing formal grounding for the architectural differences we characterize at the per-example level. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.
5	
6	\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}
7	Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear a

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \\citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \\citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \\citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \\citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \\citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \\citep{fu2023hungry} and Hyena \\citep{poli2023hyena} establish early design points in the hybrid space with gated convolutions and subquadratic operators. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \\citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \\citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \\citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \\citep{basu2026routing}. Recent theoretical work establishes a provable expressiveness hierarchy between hybrid and pure linear attention \\citep{ye2026provable}, providing formal grounding for the architectural differences we characterize at the per-example level. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.",
  "new_string": "The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \\citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \\citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \\citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \\citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \\citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention, while \\citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \\citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \\citep{basu2026routing}. Recent theoretical work establishes a provable expressiveness hierarchy between hybrid and pure linear attention \\citep{ye2026provable}, providing formal grounding for the architectural differences we characterize at the per-example level. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio."
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.",
  "new_string": "Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules, extending GLA \\citep{yang2024gla} and DeltaNet \\citep{yang2024deltanet}. These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2. xLSTM \\citep{beck2024xlstm} revives the LSTM lineage with exponential gating and matrix memory, offering an alternative approach at scale. \\citet{zimerman2025unified} show that modern gated-linear RNNs---including RetNet and HGRN---can all be cast as implicit causal self-attention, providing a unifying perspective on the family. \\citet{arora2024based} identify a recall-throughput tradeoff in linear models that motivates hybrid designs."
}
```

</details>

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}
4	The design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. Mamba-2 \citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention, while \citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \citep{basu2026routing}. Recent theoretical work establishes a provable expressiveness hierarchy between hybrid and pure linear attention \citep{ye2026provable}, providing formal grounding for the architectural differences we characterize at the per-example level. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.
5	
6	\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}
7	Our three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules, extending GLA \citep{yang2024gla} and DeltaNet \citep{yang2024deltanet}. These descend from S4 \citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, and connect to linear attention \citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2. xLSTM \citep{beck2024xlstm} revives the LSTM lineage with

For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its hybrid (+attention) variant, classifying every evaluation example as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Cross-architecture overlap of these per-example effect sets is measured via pairwise Jaccard similarity ($J \approx 0.10$), revealing architecture-specific sensitivity patterns.}
47	\label{fig:method_overview}

49	
50	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
51	
52	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
53	
54	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
55	
56	\begin{figure*}[t]
57	\centering
58	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.34\textwidth}

63	\label{fig:per_example_bars}
64	\end{minipage}%

1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\begin{table}[t]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{Model zoo summary \citep{wang2025hybrid}. All configurations share training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in backbone and attention ratio.}
9	\label{tab:model_zoo}
10	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
11	\toprule
12	Architecture & Recurrence mechanism & Scales \\
13	\midrule
14	GatedDeltaNet & Gated delta rules & 340M, 1.3B \\
15	RetNet & Multi-scale exp.\ decay & 340M, 1.3B \\
16	HGRN2 & Gated state expansion & 340M, 1.3B \\
17	\midrule
18	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Attention ratios: 0\%, 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, 25\%} \\
19	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l@{}}{\footnotesize Extended (340M only): +GLA, +DeltaNet} \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
25	
26	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (Table~\ref{tab:model_zoo}), which provides controlled comparisons: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2} at five attention ratios and two scales. The GDN 1.3B h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads vs.\ 32 for RetNet and HGRN2 (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}). We adopt the h$k$-1 notation: h3-1 = 25\% attention, h6-1 = 14.3\%, h12-1 = 7.7\%, h24-1 = 4\%. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters, isolating architecture and attention configuration. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline at both scales.
27	
28	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
29

_source line 1390 · 2026-05-25T15:18:32.960388+00:00_

108	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
109	
110	Removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA variance reduction ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the independence pattern ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate variance pattern therefore depends on GDN inclusion; the per-example stability pattern does not. Five-architecture validation at 340M (adding GLA and DeltaNet) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairwise comparisons and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}), extending the per-example divergence finding to architectures not used in the primary analysis. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}; Transformer baseline comparison in Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment}.
111

6	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
7	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}

11	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
12	
13	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
14	
15	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).

100	Each architecture's per-example response is largely idiosyncratic: observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}). This pattern generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}c): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}).
101	
102	Heuristic bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n = 3$; \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) show non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios. Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M \citep{biderman2023pythia} seed calibration yields a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-associated divergence.
103	
104	Two interpretations are consistent with the observed stability. \textbf{(a) Fingerprint view:} each recurrent backbone imposes a characteristic inductive bias that determines which examples attention can rescue. \textbf{(b) Convergence view:} even minimal attention causes all architectures to rapidly adopt Transformer-like behavior, with residual divergence from incomplete convergence. Appendix~\ref{app:transformer_entrainment} shows that h3-1 correct sets shift toward the Transformer baseline, providing partial support for (b). Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers.

17	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$ with task accuracy). This suggests multiplicity is associated with cognitive demands rather than evaluation format, though the confound cannot be fully resolved (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$), indicating architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types, confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.

_source line 1442 · 2026-05-25T15:24:36.907182+00:00_

17	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$ with task accuracy). This suggests multiplicity is associated with cognitive demands rather than evaluation format, though the confound cannot be fully resolved (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$), indicating architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types, confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.

95	\subsection{Per-Example Overlap Appears Stable from Minimal Attention}\label{ssec:decoupling}
96	
97	Having established that multiplicity varies by task type, we now ask whether it changes with the amount of attention. Does adding more attention change \emph{which} examples each architecture affects? Pairwise Jaccard appears to stabilize at the first attention level (4\%; $\bar{J} \approx 0.095$) and remains flat across all higher ratios (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}b), suggesting that minimal attention may suffice to establish the per-example divergence pattern, though the small number of ratios ($n = 5$) limits statistical confirmation. As background, LAMBADA's aggregate cross-architecture $\sigma$ shows a non-significant decreasing trend ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; MMLU reverses to $\rho = +0.6$ when excluding GDN; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). No other benchmark shows a comparable aggregate pattern ($n = 5$; negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$; Appendix

_source line 1478 · 2026-05-25T15:28:27.528809+00:00_

17	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy; Appendix~\ref{app:arceasy}) to 0.30 (LAMBADA). Format does not drive this ordering: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different answer-space sizes ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$ with task accuracy). This suggests multiplicity is associated with cognitive demands rather than evaluation format, though the confound cannot be fully resolved (Limitations). Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$), indicating architectures affect different examples regardless of how many are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$) but differs $2\times$ across task types, confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention ratio \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.

_source line 1508 · 2026-05-25T15:35:28.845348+00:00_

[飞书用户 用户]: 📐 论文自检任务：与 ACL 官方模板对齐 + 控制正文页数

请打开下面这个 ACL 官方模板，逐行、非常仔细地和我们论文目前使用的 template 对比，找出任何差异并对齐。

官方模板：
https://www.overleaf.com/latex/templates/association-for-computational-linguistics-acl-conference/jvxskxpnznfj

---

任务要求：

1. 检测任何差异
   把官方模板和我们当前论文用的 template 做完整 diff，任何差异都不能漏掉，包括但不限于：
   - 文档类参数：\documentclass[...]{acl} 的所有选项是否一致
   - preamble 中的 \usepackage：包名、版本、参数、顺序
   - 字体、字号、行距、页边距设置
   - 标题、作者、机构、邮箱区域的格式
   - Abstract 区域的环境定义
   - section / subsection / paragraph 的样式
   - 引用、参考文献的样式（natbib? bibstyle?）
   - 脚注样式
   - 表格、图片的默认样式
   - 公式编号、定理环境
   - 页眉页脚、页码
   - hyperref / url 的设置
   - 任何被我们注释掉、替换、增加的行

2. 全部向官方文档对齐
   发现任何与官方模板不一致的地方，全部改成官方模板的写法。除非有非常明确的必要原因（如 venue 要求的差异），否则不要保留任何自定义改动。如果某处差异确实有理由保留，请在汇报里单独说明原因。

3. 控制正文页数在 8 页左右
   - 正文（main text，不含 references / appendix）必须在 8 页左右
   - 严格不超过 8 页（超 1 行都不行）
   - 也不能比 8 页少太多——如果当前只有 5、6 页，说明内容不够充实，需要补充：
     * 增加实验分析 / ablation / 定性例子
     * 把过于简略的方法描述展开
     * 增加 motivation、related work、discussion 等
   - 目标是恰好填满 8 页正文

---

完成后请汇报：

(a) 我们的 template 和官方模板有哪些差异？每一处都列出来
(b) 这些差异分别是怎么修复对齐的？还是有保留的（保留的请说明原因）
(c) 修改后正文目前是几页？如果不到 8 页，准备补充哪些内容？如果超过 8 页，准备压缩哪些内容？

\documentclass[11pt]{article}

% Change "review" to "final" to generate the final (sometimes called camera-ready) version.
% Change to "preprint" to generate a non-anonymous version with page numbers.
\usepackage[review]{acl}

% Standard package includes
\usepackage{times}
\usepackage{latexsym}

% For proper rendering and hyphenation of words containing Latin characters (including in bib files)
\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
% For Vietnamese characters
% \usepackage[T5]{fontenc}
% See https://www.latex-project.org/help/documentation/encguide.pdf for other character sets

% This assumes your files are encoded as UTF8
\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}

% This is not strictly necessary, and may be commented out,
% but it will improve the layout of the manuscript,
% and will typically save some space.
\usepackage{microtype}

% This is also not strictly necessary, and may be commented out.
% However, it will improve the aesthetics of text in
% the typewriter font.
\usepackage{inconsolata}

%Including images in your LaTeX document requires adding
%additional package(s)
\usepackage{graphicx}

% If the title and author information does not fit in the area allocated, uncomment the following
%
%\setlength\titlebox{&lt;dim>}
%
% and set &lt;dim> to something 5cm or larger.

\title{Instructions for *ACL Proceedings}

\author{First Author \\

  Affiliation / Address line 1 \\

  \texttt{email@domain} \\}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

32	\title{Aggregate Equivalence $\neq$ Behavioral Equivalence: Per-Example Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Models}
33	
34	\author{Anonymous}
35	
36	\begin{document}
37	\maketitle
38	
39	\begin{abstract}
40	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures (three primary, two validation)). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that

4	Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (\S\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\% attention.
5	
6	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
7	
8	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped, hurt, or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). Binarization discards confidence magnitude, so we supplement with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).
9	
10	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Figure~\ref{fig:per_example_bars}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395; full counts in Appendix~\ref{app:per_example_counts}). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), plausibly reflecting shared sensitivity to example difficulty. 18	\title{Aggregate Equivalence $\neq$ Behavioral Equivalence: Per-Example Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Models}
19	
20	\author{Anonymous}
21	
22	\begin{document}
23	\maketitle
24	
25	\begin{abstract}
26	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures (three primary, two validation)). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at 4\% attention and remains flat across higher ratios, suggesting that minimal attention may establish which examples each architecture affects. \citet{yang2026benchmark} document analogous per-item disagreement (16--66\%) among frontier large language models (LLMs) despite comparable aggregate scores; our within-family design isolates backbone variation from training-regime differences. Confidence margins partially predict affected examples (area under the ROC curve [AUC] 0.70--0.74). Low cross-architecture overlap implies substantial complementarity, though routing-based exploitation remains task-dependent\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https
…[truncated 1788 chars]

31	
32	\author{Anonymous}
33	
34	\begin{document}
35	\maketitle
36	
37	\begin{abstract}
38	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that appear interchangeable by aggregate benchmarks can differ on which specific examples they handle correctly. Using a controlled model zoo \citep{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects of adding attention and find structured predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive}: pairwise overlap of helped and hurt example sets is uniformly low (Jaccard $\approx 0.10$, roughly twice the independence baseline; all $p < .01$ across five architectures (three primary, two validation)). This multiplicity varies by task type or evaluation format ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) rather than attention ratio. Per-example overlap appears to stabilize at
